## honest/sections/what-is-evidence.tex

Why: Prose pass: split packed sentences

- Before: Evidence is ``an event entangled, by links of cause and effect, with whatever you want to know about.'' A machine that beeps for every lottery number, winning or losing, tells you nothing: evidence must come out differently depending on the facts. Then, in a parenthesis, comes the technical version: mutual information, relative to your current uncertainty. \nb{The first definition speaks of causes in the world; the second is measured relative to what you already believe, so the same event can be evidence for one person and not for another.}

Light from your shoelaces that hits a rock leaves no trace in the rock, which is why rocks make poor witnesses in court; a photograph or a working eye keeps one, and if your eyes and brain work, you become tied to your shoelaces.

Next, rationalists value the ``paradoxical-seeming'' claim that a belief is worth holding only if something could change it. If your retina ended up in the same state whatever light entered it, you would be blind. Some belief systems, ``in a rather obvious trick,'' say that certain beliefs must be held no matter what you see; hence ``blind faith.'' A belief that does not depend on what you see blinds you as surely as losing your eyes. I do not name the belief systems or quote any of them.
- After: Evidence is ``an event entangled, by links of cause and effect, with whatever you want to know about.'' A machine that beeps for every lottery number, winning or losing, tells you nothing. Evidence must come out differently depending on the facts. A parenthesis gives the technical version: mutual information, relative to your current uncertainty. \nb{The first definition speaks of causes in the world; the second is measured relative to what you already believe, so the same event can be evidence for one person and not for another.}

Light from your shoelaces leaves no trace in a rock it hits, which is why rocks make poor witnesses in court. A photograph or a working eye keeps a trace. If your eyes and brain work, you become tied to your shoelaces.

Next, rationalists value the ``paradoxical-seeming'' claim that a belief is worth holding only if something could change it. If your retina ended up in the same state whatever light entered it, you would be blind. Some belief systems, ``in a rather obvious trick,'' say that certain beliefs must be held no matter what you see. Hence ``blind faith.'' A belief that does not depend on what you see blinds you as surely as losing your eyes. I do not name the belief systems or quote any of them.

- Before: Your beliefs, once spoken, can be evidence for someone else, since speaking and hearing are causes too: saying ``My shoelaces are untied'' over the phone shares your tie to your shoelaces with a friend. So rational beliefs are ``contagious, among honest folk who believe each other to be honest.'' Therefore a belief held for private reasons that cannot be passed on is ``suspicious.''
- After: Your beliefs, once spoken, can be evidence for someone else, since speaking and hearing are causes too. Saying ``My shoelaces are untied'' over the phone shares your tie to your shoelaces with a friend. So rational beliefs are ``contagious, among honest folk who believe each other to be honest.'' Therefore a belief held for private reasons that cannot be passed on is ``suspicious.''


## honest/sections/scientific-evidence-legal-evidence-rational-evidence.tex

Why: Prose pass: split packed sentences; replaced a signpost

- Before: Courts will not jail Wulky on the commissioner's word. My explanation: if we jailed everyone the commissioner named, we might at first catch many crime bosses, but unchecked power attracts corruption ``like honey attracts flies,'' so over time real bosses would hide better and more innocent people would be jailed. \nb{American law gives its own reason. A witness must testify from personal knowledge, and testimony must be open to cross-examination, which tests the witness's perception and memory. These are rules about reliability. The post presents them as a social convention laid on top of rationality.}

So, for a social goal, we count as legal evidence only certain kinds, such as the commissioner's own observations on a given night. Legal evidence should all be rational evidence, but not the other way round. Then science. I am wearing white socks as I write this. You may believe me, and I could testify to it in court, but it is not science, because you cannot test it yourself. Science too is a social process run by fallible people, and it wants a protected pool of especially reliable beliefs, so it adds its own strong standards. It is made of generalizations that let you ``verify for yourself that the generalization is true, without having to trust anyone's authority.'' \nb{In practice almost no one, scientists included, checks more than a sliver of it.}
- After: Courts will not jail Wulky on the commissioner's word. My explanation is this. If we jailed everyone the commissioner named, we might at first catch many crime bosses. But unchecked power attracts corruption ``like honey attracts flies.'' Over time, real bosses would hide better and more innocent people would be jailed. \nb{American law gives its own reason. A witness must testify from personal knowledge, and testimony must be open to cross-examination, which tests the witness's perception and memory. These are rules about reliability. The post presents them as a social convention laid on top of rationality.}

So, for a social goal, we count as legal evidence only certain kinds, such as the commissioner's own observations on a given night. Legal evidence should all be rational evidence, but not the other way round. Science is different again. I am wearing white socks as I write this. You may believe me, and I could testify to it in court, but it is not science, because you cannot test it yourself. Science too is a social process run by fallible people. It wants a protected pool of especially reliable beliefs, so it sets strong standards of its own. It is made of generalizations that let you ``verify for yourself that the generalization is true, without having to trust anyone's authority.'' \nb{In practice almost no one, scientists included, checks more than a sliver of it.}

- Before: Is the prediction that the Sun will rise a month from now scientific? It may seem perverse to deny it, I admit, since it follows from generalizations you can test. Then I suggest that it is not. Suppose you test an accepted theory's twenty-seventh prediction in a new setting, with no reason to doubt it. Before the test there is ``theory Q's prediction,'' but it seems dangerous to call it a scientific belief: if you already knew the scientific belief about the result, ``why bother to run the experiment?'' That is why I tie science to generalizations, which can be tested again, and not to single events, which cannot. \nb{By the same reasoning, no prediction would count as scientific before it had been tested.}

Is my definition of scientific knowledge true? ``That is not a well-formed question'': the standards are pragmatic choices. Nothing written on the stars makes p < 0.05 the standard for publication, and many argue it should be 0.01 or 0.001. \nb{So the definition is stated as a fact until it is questioned, and then it becomes a convention.}

Perhaps future generations will count as scientific only papers published in open-access journals: if you charge for knowledge, is it the knowledge of humankind, and can we fully trust a result that people must pay to criticize? I think that rule would serve science better.
- After: Is the prediction that the Sun will rise a month from now scientific? It may seem perverse to deny it, I admit, since it follows from generalizations you can test. Then I suggest that it is not. Suppose you test an accepted theory's twenty-seventh prediction in a new setting, with no reason to doubt it. Before the test there is ``theory Q's prediction,'' but it seems dangerous to call it a scientific belief. If you already knew the scientific belief about the result, ``why bother to run the experiment?'' That is why I tie science to generalizations, which can be tested again, and not to single events, which cannot. \nb{By the same reasoning, no prediction would count as scientific before it had been tested.}

Is my definition of scientific knowledge true? ``That is not a well-formed question.'' The standards are pragmatic choices. Nothing written on the stars makes p < 0.05 the standard for publication, and many argue it should be 0.01 or 0.001. \nb{So the definition is stated as a fact until it is questioned, and then it becomes a convention.}

Perhaps future generations will count as scientific only papers published in open-access journals. If you charge for knowledge, is it the knowledge of humankind? And can we fully trust a result that people must pay to criticize? I think that rule would serve science better.


## honest/sections/how-much-evidence-does-it-take.tex

Why: Prose pass: split packed sentences; replaced a signpost

- Before: A black box always beeps for the winning ticket, and beeps a quarter of the time for a losing one: a ``likelihood ratio'' of 4 to 1. Tried on every ticket, it beeps for about 33 million losers. That does not win the lottery, but it improves your odds from 1 in 131 million to 1 in 33 million. Two such boxes, independent of each other, cut that to about 8 million. Evidence 131 million times more likely for the winner than for a loser leaves you with the winner and, on average, one loser, so only an even chance. The clean way to see it is in odds: start at 1 to 131,115,984, and each box multiplies the odds by 4.
- After: A black box always beeps for the winning ticket, and beeps a quarter of the time for a losing one: a ``likelihood ratio'' of 4 to 1. Tried on every ticket, it beeps for about 33 million losers. That does not win the lottery, but it improves your odds from 1 in 131 million to 1 in 33 million. Two such boxes, independent of each other, cut that to about 8 million. Evidence 131 million times more likely for the winner than for a loser leaves you with the winner and, on average, one loser, so only an even chance. It is cleanest in odds. You start at 1 to 131,115,984, and each box multiplies the odds by 4.

- Before: Then the general rule: the more possibilities there are, the less likely the hypothesis ``seems a priori,'' and the surer you want to be, the more evidence you need. \nb{In the lottery the starting probability was given exactly. In any real question, setting it is the hard part, and the post's only word on it is ``seems.''}
- After: The general rule follows. The more possibilities there are, the less likely the hypothesis ``seems a priori.'' And the surer you want to be, the more evidence you need. \nb{In the lottery the starting probability was given exactly. In any real question, setting it is the hard part, and the post's only word on it is ``seems.''}


## honest/sections/einstein-s-arrogance.tex

Why: Prose pass: split packed sentences

- Before: That sounds foolhardy. ``Who can know that the theory is correct, in advance of experimental test?'' Of course, Einstein turned out to be right, and I try not to criticize people when they are right: if they deserve criticism, an occasion when they are wrong will come soon enough.

My defense uses the arithmetic of my previous post. To pick the correct hypothesis out of 100 million, you need about 27 bits of evidence; a test with a one-in-a-million false-positive rate, about 20 bits, would leave a hundred candidates. Traditional Rationality pictures a hunch that comes first and evidence gathered afterwards to justify it to others. On the Bayesian view the evidence is needed before any justifying, ``just to locate the hypothesis in theory-space,'' and hunches obey the same rule. \nb{Charles Peirce made this counting argument in 1903: out of ``trillions of trillions of hypotheses,'' the physicist hits on the right one within a dozen guesses, which chance cannot explain. He concluded that the guessing faculty is ``not strong enough to be oftener right than wrong.'' The post does not mention him.}
- After: That sounds foolhardy. ``Who can know that the theory is correct, in advance of experimental test?'' Einstein turned out to be right, and I try not to criticize people when they are right. If they deserve criticism, an occasion when they are wrong will come soon enough.

My defense uses the arithmetic of my previous post. To pick the correct hypothesis out of 100 million, you need about 27 bits of evidence. A test with a one-in-a-million false-positive rate gives about 20 bits, and would leave a hundred candidates. Traditional Rationality pictures a hunch that comes first and evidence gathered afterwards to justify it to others. On the Bayesian view the evidence is needed before any justifying, ``just to locate the hypothesis in theory-space,'' and hunches obey the same rule. \nb{Charles Peirce made this counting argument in 1903: out of ``trillions of trillions of hypotheses,'' the physicist hits on the right one within a dozen guesses, which chance cannot explain. He concluded that the guessing faculty is ``not strong enough to be oftener right than wrong.'' The post does not mention him.}


## honest/sections/occam-s-razor.tex

Why: Prose pass: split packed sentences in the account of fit and complexity

- Before: So the length of an English sentence is a poor measure, and ``fitting'' the facts by merely not forbidding them is not enough. Words are labels for things the listener already knows, and a short label, ``Fnord!'', does not make a claim simple, since you must first tell the listener what it stands for. ``Witch'' and ``anger'' sound simple only because we do not see the brain's machinery behind them. To a human, Maxwell's equations take longer to explain than Thor. But a program that simulates Maxwell's equations is ``enormously'' shorter than one that simulates a mind, ``as it turns out.''
- After: So the length of an English sentence is a poor measure, and ``fitting'' the facts by merely not forbidding them is not enough. Words are labels for things the listener already knows. A short label, ``Fnord!'', does not make a claim simple, since you must first tell the listener what it stands for. ``Witch'' and ``anger'' sound simple only because we do not see the brain's machinery behind them. To a human, Maxwell's equations take longer to explain than Thor. But a program that simulates Maxwell's equations is ``enormously'' shorter than one that simulates a mind, ``as it turns out.''

- Before: In the version I prefer, programs assign probabilities to data, and fit means giving the observed data high probability. Probabilities sum to 1, so a program can fit one outcome better only by fitting others worse; there is no coin that gives heads and tails 100\% each. Judge by fit alone and you always prefer a program that predicts the exact data: one fixed to show HTTHHT fits that sequence 64 times better than a fair coin. Judge by simplicity alone and the fair coin always wins, even for HTHHTHHHTHHHHT..., where a slightly more complex hypothesis fits far better. So fit must be paid for with complexity: each bit stored in the program must buy at least a doubling of fit, or ``you will sooner or later decide that all fair coins are fixed.'' Moving a bit from the data into the program gains nothing unless the program compresses the data. Solomonoff's predictor weights every program by 2 to the minus its length and by its fit, and it is uncomputable. \nb{This is the post's answer to how complexity can be measured. Apart from the comparison of Maxwell and Thor, the post gives no way to estimate it for a real explanation.} Minimum Message Length is nearly the same: send a code, then the data in that code, and prefer the shortest total.

Now the witch. To tell a friend the sequence 0101010101, you would still have to send all ten digits after saying ``witch.'' Witchcraft, like phlogiston, permits everything, so it compresses nothing; it is ``a useless prologue.'' The trick was in the word ``it.''

Apart from the comparison of Maxwell and Thor, the post gives no way to estimate it for a real explanation.} Minimum Message Length is nearly the same. You send a code, then the data in that code, and you prefer the shortest total.

Now the witch. To tell a friend the sequence 0101010101, you would still have to send all ten digits after saying ``witch.'' Witchcraft, like phlogiston, permits everything, so it compresses nothing. It is ``a useless prologue.'' The trick was in the word ``it.''


## honest/sections/your-strength-as-a-rationalist.tex

Why: Prose pass: split packed sentences; replaced two signposts

- Before: Perhaps I should have realized that a friend of a stranger in a chatroom is less reliable than a published journal article. But belief is easier than disbelief: we believe by instinct, and disbelief takes effort. I had all the information I needed, I even noticed the problem, and I ignored it.

Then the principle: a model is useful for what it cannot explain, and a model that forbids nothing tells you nothing. \nb{This is true, and it is Karl Popper's idea.}

Then the thesis: ``Your strength as a rationalist is your ability to be more confused by fiction than by reality.'' If you can explain any outcome equally well, you know nothing. \nb{A true report that clashes with a wrong model is just as confusing as a lie, and the feeling does not say which it is.}

## honest/sections/absence-of-evidence-is-evidence-of-absence.tex

Why: Prose pass: split packed sentences; replaced a signpost

- Before: Evidence shifts probability toward the hypotheses that made it more likely, relative to the others; a hypothesis can give the evidence a high likelihood and still lose ground to one that gives it a higher one. A secret Fifth Column might delay its sabotage, but no sabotage is still more likely if there is no Fifth Column at all. So the absence of sabotage counts against a Fifth Column. \nb{Warren said the sabotage was being timed like Pearl Harbor, for one coordinated day. If so, no sabotage so far was nearly as likely with a Fifth Column as without one, and it told you almost nothing.}

Then the notation, and the theorem: if seeing something would raise the probability of a hypothesis, then not seeing it must lower it, since the probability before looking is a weighted average of the two possible probabilities after, and so lies between them.

I grant that absence of proof is not proof of absence: in logic, ``A implies B'' does not give ``not-A implies not-B.'' \nb{That is what people usually mean by the slogan the title answers, ``absence of evidence is not evidence of absence.'' The post quotes no one who uses the slogan, and the title defeats it by reading ``evidence'' in the technical sense of probability theory, not the sense its users intend.}

Absence may be ``strong evidence of absence or very weak evidence of absence,'' depending on how likely the cause was to leave signs. Gaps in the fossil record are very weak evidence, because fossils rarely form, and it is futile to point to them when many strong positive finds are on record. If there are no positive observations at all, though, it is time to worry; hence the Fermi paradox, the absence of any sign of aliens. \nb{The post does not apply this to Warren.}

I repeat the lesson of ``Your Strength as a Rationalist'': a model's strength lies in what it cannot explain. If you do not notice when your model makes the evidence unlikely, you might as well have no model and no evidence.
- After: Evidence shifts probability toward the hypotheses that made it more likely, relative to the others. A hypothesis can give the evidence a high likelihood and still lose ground to one that gives it a higher one. A secret Fifth Column might delay its sabotage, but no sabotage is still more likely if there is no Fifth Column at all. So the absence of sabotage counts against a Fifth Column. \nb{Warren said the sabotage was being timed like Pearl Harbor, for one coordinated day. If so, no sabotage so far was nearly as likely with a Fifth Column as without one, and it told you almost nothing.}

I then give the notation and a theorem. If seeing something would raise the probability of a hypothesis, then not seeing it must lower it. The probability before looking is a weighted average of the two possible probabilities after, so it lies between them.

I grant that absence of proof is not proof of absence. In logic, ``A implies B'' does not give ``not-A implies not-B.'' \nb{That is what people usually mean by the slogan the title answers, ``absence of evidence is not evidence of absence.'' The post quotes no one who uses the slogan, and the title defeats it by reading ``evidence'' in the technical sense of probability theory, not the sense its users intend.}

If there are no positive observations at all, though, it is time to worry. Hence the Fermi paradox, the absence of any sign of aliens. \nb{The post does not apply this to Warren.}

I repeat the lesson of ``Your Strength as a Rationalist.'' A model's strength lies in what it cannot explain. If you do not notice when your model makes the evidence unlikely, you might as well have no model and no evidence.


## honest/sections/conservation-of-expected-evidence.tex

Why: Prose pass: split packed sentences; replaced signposts

- Before: In 1631 the priest Friedrich Spee described how accused witches were judged. A bad life proved guilt, and so did a good one, because witches pretend to be virtuous. Fear in prison proved guilt, and so did calm. If she fled when accused, that proved guilt; if she stayed, the devil had held her there. Spee heard many confessions, so he could see every branch: whatever the woman did, it counted against her. In any one case you would hear only one branch. \nb{Spee saw the point without any probability theory.}

It is for this reason that scientists write down their predictions in advance. \nb{In 2007, outside clinical trials, few scientists registered their predictions publicly before collecting data.}

Then my law, which I name ``Conservation of Expected Evidence'': your expected belief after seeing the evidence must equal your belief before. I write out three lines. \nb{The third is the law of total probability, taught in any first course in the subject.} Then comes the slogan: ``for every expectation of evidence, there is an equal and opposite expectation of counterevidence.''
- After: In 1631 the priest Friedrich Spee described how accused witches were judged. A bad life proved guilt, and so did a good one, because witches pretend to be virtuous. Fear in prison proved guilt, and so did calm. If she fled when accused, that proved guilt. If she stayed, the devil had held her there. Spee heard many confessions, so he could see every branch. Whatever the woman did, it counted against her. In any one case you would hear only one branch. \nb{Spee saw the point without any probability theory.}

This is why scientists write down their predictions in advance. \nb{In 2007, outside clinical trials, few scientists registered their predictions publicly before collecting data.}

I name my law ``Conservation of Expected Evidence.'' Your expected belief after seeing the evidence must equal your belief before. I write out three lines. \nb{The third is the law of total probability, taught in any first course in the subject.} In a slogan: ``for every expectation of evidence, there is an equal and opposite expectation of counterevidence.''

- Before: ``For a true Bayesian, it is impossible to seek evidence that confirms a theory''; you can only seek evidence that tests it. \nb{In plain English you can: a few paragraphs earlier the post described a test a theory will probably pass. The next sentence narrows the claim to raising your confidence on average.}

This ``can take quite a load off your mind,'' since you need not plan how to make the evidence confirm your theory. Weakening what a surprising result would count against you can only be done by weakening, exactly as much, what an expected result would count for you; it is zero-sum. ``You might as well sit back and relax while you wait for the evidence to come in.'' \nb{That is advice for an ideal reasoner. A human can reinterpret results to suit a theory, and often does.}

You can only seek evidence that tests it. \nb{In plain English you can: a few paragraphs earlier the post described a test a theory will probably pass. ## honest/sections/hindsight-devalues-science.tex

Why: Prose pass: split packed sentences

- Before: Daphna Baratz gave students pairs of findings, one true and one its opposite, such as ``In prosperous times people spend a larger portion of their income than during a recession,'' and they rated each as what they ``would have predicted.'' This is why people think they do not need science.

Hindsight makes us undervalue how surprising findings are, ``especially the discoveries we understand,'' and if you know neurology or physics you ``probably'' underestimate the surprise there too. I give no evidence for this. The cost is that we undervalue the researchers' work and, worse, fail to notice evidence that does not fit what we really would have expected. \nb{Studies of experts and hindsight bias disagree: one meta-analysis found experts less prone to it, a later and larger one found no relation.}
- After: Daphna Baratz gave students pairs of findings, one true and one its opposite. One was ``In prosperous times people spend a larger portion of their income than during a recession.'' The students rated each finding, true or reversed, as what they ``would have predicted.'' This is why people think they do not need science.

Hindsight makes us undervalue how surprising findings are, ``especially the discoveries we understand.'' If you know neurology or physics you ``probably'' underestimate the surprise there too. I give no evidence for this. The cost is that we undervalue the researchers' work and, worse, fail to notice evidence that does not fit what we really would have expected. \nb{Studies of experts and hindsight bias disagree: one meta-analysis found experts less prone to it, a later and larger one found no relation.}


## honest/sections/illusion-of-transparency-why-no-one-understands-you.tex

Why: Prose pass: split packed sentences; replaced signposts

- Before: After a paragraph on hindsight bias: ``We always know what we mean by our words, and so we expect others to know it too.'' I call this the illusion of transparency. Rereading our own words, we find the meaning we intended falls into place, and it is hard to put ourselves in the place of someone who has only the words. It is close to hindsight bias: once we know, we cannot un-know. I report several studies.
- After: I begin with a paragraph on hindsight bias, then state the idea: ``We always know what we mean by our words, and so we expect others to know it too.'' I call this the illusion of transparency. Rereading our own words, we find the meaning we intended falls into place. It is hard to put ourselves in the place of someone who has only the words. It is close to hindsight bias: once we know, we cannot un-know. I report several studies.

- Before: In a third, speakers said ambiguous sentences such as ``The man is chasing a woman on a bicycle.'' They thought they were understood in 72\% of cases and were understood in 61\%. When the listener had not understood, speakers thought they had in 46\% of cases; when the listener had understood, speakers thought not in only 12\%. The same study had a second finding: people who overheard the speakers did not overestimate, expecting listeners to understand in only 56\% of cases. \nb{The overhearers also knew the intended meaning. So knowing what you mean is not enough to cause the illusion, though the post's definition says that it is. The authors put the bias more narrowly, in speakers judging ``their own utterances.'' The post reports the overhearers in one sentence and moves on.}

Then history. Following Keysar and Barr: ``two days before Germany's attack on Poland,'' Chamberlain sent Hitler a letter meant to say that Britain would fight, and Hitler heard it as conciliatory. \nb{The letter was sent ten days before the attack. It says the British government is ``resolved, and prepared, to employ without delay all the forces at their command.'' Hitler's reply restates the commitment and says, ``I take note of this statement of yours.'' On the documents, he understood the letter and attacked anyway. So the post's one example from outside the laboratory is of a message that was understood.}

``Be not too quick to blame those who misunderstand your perfectly clear sentences'': your words are probably more ambiguous than you think. \nb{The sentences in the bicycle study were chosen for being ambiguous. It shows that speakers overrate ambiguous sentences, not how often everyday sentences are ambiguous.}

- After: In a third, speakers said ambiguous sentences such as ``The man is chasing a woman on a bicycle.'' They thought they were understood in 72\% of cases and were understood in 61\%. When the listener had not understood, speakers thought they had in 46\% of cases. When the listener had understood, speakers thought not in only 12\%. The same study had a second finding. People who overheard the speakers did not overestimate. They expected listeners to understand in only 56\% of cases. \nb{The overhearers also knew the intended meaning. So knowing what you mean is not enough to cause the illusion, though the post's definition says that it is. The authors put the bias more narrowly, in speakers judging ``their own utterances.'' The post reports the overhearers in one sentence and moves on.}

My one example from history follows Keysar and Barr. Chamberlain, ``two days before Germany's attack on Poland,'' sent Hitler a letter meant to say that Britain would fight. Hitler heard it as conciliatory. \nb{The letter was sent ten days before the attack. It says the British government is ``resolved, and prepared, to employ without delay all the forces at their command.'' Hitler's reply restates the commitment and says, ``I take note of this statement of yours.'' On the documents, he understood the letter and attacked anyway. So the post's one example from outside the laboratory is of a message that was understood.}

``Be not too quick to blame those who misunderstand your perfectly clear sentences.'' Your words are probably more ambiguous than you think. \nb{The sentences in the bicycle study were chosen for being ambiguous. It shows that speakers overrate ambiguous sentences, not how often everyday sentences are ambiguous.}

And do not hint that you are a dozen steps ahead of them, or ``They'll just think you're condescending.''

## honest/sections/fake-explanations.tex

Why: Prose pass: split packed sentences

- Before: I start with a joke. An instructor shows physics students a metal plate next to a radiator. The side near the radiator feels cool and the far side warm. The students suggest convection, strange metals and other causes; none says ``I don't know'' or ``This seems impossible.'' The instructor had turned the plate around.

Take the student who stammers, ``Eh, maybe because of the heat conduction and so?'' The words are easy to say loudly; do they control what the student expects? Put ``phlogiston'' or ``magic'' after ``because of'' instead. ``Heat conduction'' is what Spock would say, and ``magic'' what Giles would say; the phrases belong to different ``literary genres.'' A Bayesian ignores genre: what matters in a model is how it constrains what you expect. \nb{Richard Feynman made the same comparison. In \textsc{Surely You're Joking, Mr. Feynman!} (1985) he wrote of a textbook's ``Energy makes it go'': ``Suppose it's `Wakalixes.' That's the general principle: `Wakalixes makes it go.' There's no knowledge coming in.'' The post does not mention him.}

Heat conduction normally leads you to expect the near side to be warmer. If it can also explain the near side being cooler, it can explain ``pretty much anything.'' \nb{With the right starting condition it does explain a cooler near side, and the post's own physicist, below, explains the plate that way. What the student's answer lacks is the starting condition.} An answer that fits any outcome carries ``zero knowledge''; I call ``heat conduction,'' used this way, ``a disguised hypothesis of maximum entropy.'' \nb{The principle is Karl Popper's.}
- After: I start with a joke. An instructor shows physics students a metal plate next to a radiator. The side near the radiator feels cool and the far side warm. The students suggest convection, strange metals and other causes. None says ``I don't know'' or ``This seems impossible.'' The instructor had turned the plate around.

Take the student who stammers, ``Eh, maybe because of the heat conduction and so?'' The words are easy to say loudly. But do they control what the student expects? There's no knowledge coming in.'' The post does not mention him.}

If it can also explain the near side being cooler, it can explain ``pretty much anything.'' \nb{With the right starting condition it does explain a cooler near side, and the post's own physicist, below, explains the plate that way. What the student's answer lacks is the starting condition.} An answer that fits any outcome carries ``zero knowledge.'' ## honest/sections/guessing-the-teacher-s-password.tex

Why: Prose pass: split packed sentences

- Before: We tend to think that a student who answers ``Waves!'' has made a true statement, since we accept the same word from a physicist. That is a bad habit learned at school. Words have no intrinsic definitions: if I hear ``beaver'' and think of a large rodent, that is a fact about my mind, not about the syllables. ``Made of waves'' can call up a hypothesis in someone's mind, but the syllables themselves are neither right nor wrong. School gives gold stars for saying them, so students come to think that what they say has a truth-value of its own. \nb{On a standard view in philosophy of language, from Hilary Putnam and Tyler Burge, a speaker who uses a word as the experts do, and would accept their correction, means what the experts mean. On that view the student's answer says what the physicist's says, and what the student lacks is understanding.}

Ask a student why the far side of a metal plate is warmer. ``I don't know'' earns no gold star, so the student offers one of the teacher's phrases: ``Eh, maybe because of heat conduction?'' This is not a hypothesis about the plate; it is ``an attempt to guess the teacher's password.'' Unlearning the habit starts with noticing the difference between an explanation and a password. \nb{Feynman described the same thing in \textsc{Surely You're Joking, Mr. Feynman!}: Brazilian students who could define Brewster's angle but did not recognize it in the light off the bay had ``memorized everything, but they didn't know what anything meant.''}
- After: We tend to think that a student who answers ``Waves!'' has made a true statement, since we accept the same word from a physicist. That is a bad habit learned at school. Words have no intrinsic definitions. If I hear ``beaver'' and think of a large rodent, that is a fact about my mind, not about the syllables. ``Made of waves'' can call up a hypothesis in someone's mind, but the syllables themselves are neither right nor wrong. School gives gold stars for saying them, so students come to think that what they say has a truth-value of its own. \nb{On a standard view in philosophy of language, from Hilary Putnam and Tyler Burge, a speaker who uses a word as the experts do, and would accept their correction, means what the experts mean. On that view the student's answer says what the physicist's says, and what the student lacks is understanding.}

Ask a student why the far side of a metal plate is warmer. ``I don't know'' earns no gold star, so the student offers one of the teacher's phrases: ``Eh, maybe because of heat conduction?'' This is not a hypothesis about the plate. It is ``an attempt to guess the teacher's password.'' Unlearning the habit starts with noticing the difference between an explanation and a password. \nb{Feynman described the same thing in \textsc{Surely You're Joking, Mr. Feynman!}: Brazilian students who could define Brewster's angle but did not recognize it in the light off the bay had ``memorized everything, but they didn't know what anything meant.''}

Why: Prose pass: one claim of the theory per sentence; split packed sentences

- Before: Phlogiston was the eighteenth century's answer to what fire is and why wood becomes ash, ``and that was it.'' Phlogiston escaped from burning things as visible fire, leaving ash, the ``true material''; flames in closed containers went out because the air was saturated with phlogiston; charcoal left little residue because it was nearly pure phlogiston. But no one used it to predict: ``You looked at the result first,'' and then explained it. ``It could explain everything.'' \nb{The historical record is more mixed. The theory forbade outcomes: burned metals should have lost phlogiston, and the weight some of them gained counted against it. In 1783 Priestley predicted, and then saw, that a metal calx heated in inflammable air, which he took for phlogiston, would turn back into metal. The post's charge fits the theory's later years, when its defenders adjusted it to each new finding.} Fake explanations do not feel fake; that is the danger.

``Modern research suggests'' that people reason about causes with something like the graphs of Bayes nets. \nb{Such research existed, for example Gopnik and colleagues' 2004 account of children's causal learning. The post cites none.} Rain makes the sidewalk wet, and a wet sidewalk is slippery. If we already know the sidewalk is wet, learning that it is slippery tells us nothing more about rain. Phlogiston makes fire hot and bright, and that feels like an explanation too, because the mind stores it in the same form. It does not notice when a cause's arrow constrains nothing, and hindsight makes a cause fitted to its effect feel as if it predicted it.
- After: Phlogiston was the eighteenth century's answer to what fire is and why wood becomes ash, ``and that was it.'' Phlogiston escaped from burning things as visible fire, leaving ash, the ``true material.'' Flames in closed containers went out because the air was saturated with phlogiston. Charcoal left little residue because it was nearly pure phlogiston. But no one used it to predict. ``You looked at the result first,'' and then explained it. ``It could explain everything.'' \nb{The historical record is more mixed. The theory forbade outcomes: burned metals should have lost phlogiston, and the weight some of them gained counted against it. In 1783 Priestley predicted, and then saw, that a metal calx heated in inflammable air, which he took for phlogiston, would turn back into metal. The post's charge fits the theory's later years, when its defenders adjusted it to each new finding.} Fake explanations do not feel fake, and that is the danger.

``Modern research suggests'' that people reason about causes with something like the graphs of Bayes nets. \nb{Such research existed, for example Gopnik and colleagues' 2004 account of children's causal learning. The post cites none.} Rain makes the sidewalk wet, and a wet sidewalk is slippery. If we already know the sidewalk is wet, learning that it is slippery tells us nothing more about rain. Phlogiston makes fire hot and bright, and that feels like an explanation too, because the mind stores it in the same form. It does not notice when a cause's arrow constrains nothing. And hindsight makes a cause fitted to its effect feel as if it predicted it.

- Before: The phlogiston theorists were not fools, and your own fake explanations will not be labeled. So it is not enough to check how well a theory fits facts you already know: ``You've got to predict for tomorrow, not yesterday.'' It is ``the only way.'' \nb{Old facts have counted as strong evidence when a theory was not fitted to them. Mercury's perihelion, known for decades, counted for general relativity at least as much as its new prediction of light bending. Yudkowsky later wrote that Einstein used Mercury only ``for verification of his answer produced by other means.''}

- After: The phlogiston theorists were not fools, and your own fake explanations will not be labeled. So it is not enough to check how well a theory fits facts you already know. ``You've got to predict for tomorrow, not yesterday.'' It is ``the only way.'' \nb{Old facts have counted as strong evidence when a theory was not fitted to them. Mercury's perihelion, known for decades, counted for general relativity at least as much as its new prediction of light bending. Yudkowsky later wrote that Einstein used Mercury only ``for verification of his answer produced by other means.''}



## honest/sections/semantic-stopsigns.tex

Why: Prose pass: split packed sentences; removed a 'not X but Y' set-up

- Before: Science traces events back to the Big Bang, but the physical laws still seem to call for an explanation; if we are a computer simulation, the computer runs on some other world's laws, and where did those come from? At this point some people say ``God!'' I ask what could make anyone think this even helped, since the next question is ``Where did God come from?'' Saying that God is uncaused, or created Himself, leaves us just where ``Time began with the Big Bang'' did: we can still ask why the whole system exists, or ``why some events but not others are allowed to be uncaused.'' \nb{Defenders of the cosmological argument answer that question: only what is contingent, or what begins to exist, needs a cause. The reply is disputed, and the post does not mention it.}

I am not discussing the First Cause itself, only asking why anyone thinks ``God!'' resolves it. My answer is that saying ``God!'' ``is a way of belonging to a tribe,'' so people say it as often as they can, even about why a hurricane struck New Orleans. I give no evidence for this. But even if God exists, saying so leaves the First Cause as puzzling as before; how could anyone not notice?

Jonathan Wallace suggested that ``God!'' is a semantic stopsign: not a claim, but a signal not to think past this point. \nb{Wallace used the phrase ``semantic stop-sign'' in an essay written after the September 11 attacks, and made the same point in 1995 with a similar chain of questions about a made-up creator called ``Og.''}
- After: Science traces events back to the Big Bang, but the physical laws still seem to call for an explanation. If we are a computer simulation, the computer runs on some other world's laws. Where did those come from? At this point some people say ``God!'' I ask what could make anyone think this even helped, since the next question is ``Where did God come from?'' Saying that God is uncaused, or created Himself, leaves us just where ``Time began with the Big Bang'' did. We can still ask why the whole system exists, or ``why some events but not others are allowed to be uncaused.'' \nb{Defenders of the cosmological argument answer that question: only what is contingent, or what begins to exist, needs a cause. The reply is disputed, and the post does not mention it.}

I am not discussing the First Cause itself, only asking why anyone thinks ``God!'' resolves it. My answer is that saying ``God!'' ``is a way of belonging to a tribe,'' so people say it as often as they can, even about why a hurricane struck New Orleans. I give no evidence for this. But even if God exists, saying so leaves the First Cause as puzzling as before. How could anyone not notice?

Jonathan Wallace suggested that ``God!'' is a semantic stopsign. It makes no claim. It signals that you should not think past this point. \nb{Wallace used the phrase ``semantic stop-sign'' in an essay written after the September 11 attacks, and made the same point in 1995 with a similar chain of questions about a made-up creator called ``Og.''}

- Before: A caution: do not turn ``it's just a stopsign'' into a new all-purpose counterargument. No word is a stopsign in itself; it depends on the person, and strong feelings about something do not make it one. The word ``intelligence'' once was one for me. What marks a stopsign is ``failure to consider the obvious next question.'' \nb{By this test, a theist who gives the reply described in the earlier note has considered the obvious next question, and so is not using a stopsign, though the post offered ``God!'' as ``the obvious first example.''}

- After: A caution: do not turn ``it's just a stopsign'' into a new all-purpose counterargument. No word is a stopsign in itself. It depends on the person, and strong feelings about something do not make it one. The word ``intelligence'' once was one for me. What marks a stopsign is ``failure to consider the obvious next question.'' \nb{By this test, a theist who gives the reply described in the earlier note has considered the obvious next question, and so is not using a stopsign, though the post offered ``God!'' as ``the obvious first example.''}



## honest/sections/mysterious-answers-to-mysterious-questions.tex

Why: Prose pass: split packed sentences; the four signs one per sentence

- Before: This was vitalism: an ``Élan vital'' made living matter move and took part in chemistry that non-living matter could not do, until Wöhler's synthesis of urea ``was a major blow.'' \nb{The historian Douglas McKie called the major blow ``a chemical legend'': chemists went on asserting a vital force after the synthesis of 1828.} ``Élan vital'' was a curiosity-stopper. It gives the feeling of knowing why your hand moves, but it predicts nothing, not even whether the hand will make heat, and it fits every outcome. \nb{By the post's own account, vitalism forbade some chemistry outside living things, which is why a synthesis could count against it. A theory that a result can count against does not fit every outcome.}
- After: This was vitalism. An ``Élan vital'' made living matter move and took part in chemistry that non-living matter could not do. Then Wöhler's synthesis of urea ``was a major blow.'' \nb{The historian Douglas McKie called the major blow ``a chemical legend'': chemists went on asserting a vital force after the synthesis of 1828.} ``Élan vital'' was a curiosity-stopper. It gives the feeling of knowing why your hand moves. But it predicts nothing, not even whether the hand will make heat, and it fits every outcome. \nb{By the post's own account, vitalism forbade some chemistry outside living things, which is why a synthesis could count against it. A theory that a result can count against does not fit every outcome.}

- Before: Then my principle: ``ignorance exists in the map, not in the territory.'' Mystery is a fact about a mind, not about a phenomenon. \nb{In March 2008, in ``Probability is in the Mind,'' Yudkowsky attributed nearly the same sentence to E. T. Jaynes.} Vitalism and phlogiston put a mystery into a substance, as the physician in Molière explains a sleeping draught by its ``dormitive potency.'' Neither made any outcome easier to explain than another; each wrapped the question in ``a small, hard, opaque black ball.'' Faced with a mystery, we more readily posit a mysterious substance than a complex process underneath. The deeper error is to think an answer can be mysterious: the vitalists saw a gap in their knowledge and plugged it with mysterious stuff, confusing the map with the territory. ``Mystery is a property of questions, not answers.'' This is ``the ultimate and fully general explanation'' of why people are shocked, again and again, to find that mysterious questions have non-mysterious answers. I describe no such shock.

I call such theories ``mysterious answers to mysterious questions,'' and give four signs: the answer stops curiosity; it has no moving parts, being a blank substance or force whose workings are never spelled out; those who give it cherish their ignorance; and afterward the phenomenon is as mysterious as before.

- After: My principle is that ``ignorance exists in the map, not in the territory.'' Mystery is a fact about a mind, not about a phenomenon. \nb{In March 2008, in ``Probability is in the Mind,'' Yudkowsky attributed nearly the same sentence to E. T. Jaynes.} Vitalism and phlogiston put a mystery into a substance, as the physician in Molière explains a sleeping draught by its ``dormitive potency.'' Neither made any outcome easier to explain than another. Each wrapped the question in ``a small, hard, opaque black ball.'' Faced with a mystery, we more readily posit a mysterious substance than a complex process underneath. The deeper error is to think an answer can be mysterious. The vitalists saw a gap in their knowledge and plugged it with mysterious stuff, confusing the map with the territory. ``Mystery is a property of questions, not answers.'' This is ``the ultimate and fully general explanation'' of why people are shocked, again and again, to find that mysterious questions have non-mysterious answers. I describe no such shock.

I call such theories ``mysterious answers to mysterious questions,'' and give four signs. The answer stops curiosity. It has no moving parts, being a blank substance or force whose workings are never spelled out. Those who give it cherish their ignorance. And afterward the phenomenon is as mysterious as before.



## honest/sections/the-futility-of-emergence.tex

Why: Prose pass: the signs of a mysterious answer one per sentence

- Before: I object to the noun, not the verb. ``X emerges from Y'' is fine if Y is a specific model, as gravity arises from the curvature of spacetime in general relativity, or chemistry from atoms in quantum electrodynamics. Saying that gravity depends on ``arisence'' would be no explanation. But people commonly use ``emergence'' as an explanation in itself. I have ``lost track'' of how often I have heard ``Intelligence is an emergent phenomenon!'' I quote no one. The phrase has every sign of a mysterious answer: it makes no new prediction about real minds, it satisfies curiosity without feeding it, it has no moving parts, those who say it ``confess their ignorance of the internals, and take pride in it,'' and intelligence stays as mysterious as before.
- After: I object to the noun, not the verb. ``X emerges from Y'' is fine if Y is a specific model, as gravity arises from the curvature of spacetime in general relativity, or chemistry from atoms in quantum electrodynamics. Saying that gravity depends on ``arisence'' would be no explanation. But people commonly use ``emergence'' as an explanation in itself. I have ``lost track'' of how often I have heard ``Intelligence is an emergent phenomenon!'' I quote no one. The phrase has every sign of a mysterious answer. It makes no new prediction about real minds. It satisfies curiosity without feeding it. It has no moving parts. Those who say it ``confess their ignorance of the internals, and take pride in it.'' And intelligence stays as mysterious as before.

- Before: Emergence is popular because it is ``the junk food of curiosity.'' I give no evidence for this. Humans are still humans even after a few science classes in college: freed from settled science, they do what their ancestors did, dressed in the literary genre of science.

- After: Emergence is popular because it is ``the junk food of curiosity.'' I give no evidence for this. Humans are still humans even after a few science classes in college. Freed from settled science, they do what their ancestors did, dressed in the literary genre of science.



## honest/sections/positive-bias-look-into-the-dark.tex

Why: Prose pass: split packed sentences; removed the reporting tag 'I add' and a signpost

- Before: The task is Peter Wason's. Subjects are usually confident, yet only about a fifth find the rule, in the original and in replications. Most test triplets that their own guess predicts will fit, hear ``Yes,'' and announce the guess: someone who guesses ``increasing by two'' tests 8-10-12, and someone who guesses X, 2X, 3X tests 3-6-9. Some announce rules far more complicated than the real one. To find the real rule, you must test triplets that should not fit, such as 20-23-26.
- After: The task is Peter Wason's. Subjects are usually confident, yet only about a fifth find the rule, in the original and in replications. Most test triplets that their own guess predicts will fit, hear ``Yes,'' and announce the guess. Someone who guesses ``increasing by two'' tests 8-10-12. Someone who guesses X, 2X, 3X tests 3-6-9. Some announce rules far more complicated than the real one. To find the real rule, you must test triplets that should not fit, such as 20-23-26.

- Before: Then comes phlogiston, which could explain a flame going out and could just as well have explained it not going out.
- After: Phlogiston is my next example. It could explain a flame going out, and could just as well have explained it not going out.

- Before: The 2-4-6 task is cold logic, not emotional, and still people fail. The mistake is below the level of words, in which example springs to mind, so knowing in words that you should test both kinds will not by itself fix it; you have to learn, wordlessly, ``to flinch toward the zero, instead of away from it.'' \nb{Later research, reported by Gale and Ball in 2006, found that rewording the task, asking subjects to find two complementary rules instead of one, raised success from about a fifth to over 60 per cent. That does not show that teaching the rule works, but it shows the error depends on how the task is framed, not only on instinct. The post reports only the fifth.} I remind you that I have long argued that ``the strength of a hypothesis is what it can't explain, not what it can.''

So to judge an explanation, look for results it could not explain. A commenter offered superconductivity and ferromagnetism as examples of emergence; I replied that their absence would be examples of emergence too, which was the problem. Then I confess that I too fell for the 2-4-6 task the first time I read about it. I add: ``I'm still working on it myself.''

So much of this skill is below words that people agree with you and then, in the next sentence, do the opposite; I write partly to see what my words have not conveyed. Are you, right now, searching only for positive examples of positive bias?

- After: The 2-4-6 task is cold logic, not emotional, and still people fail. The mistake is below the level of words, in which example springs to mind. The post reports only the fifth.} I remind you that I have long argued that ``the strength of a hypothesis is what it can't explain, not what it can.''

## honest/sections/lawful-uncertainty.tex

Why: Prose pass: split packed sentences; removed the reporting tag 'I add'

- Before: I quote Robyn Dawes on an old kind of experiment. Seventy per cent of the cards are blue, in random order. Always predicting blue is right 70\% of the time. Subjects instead predict blue about 70\% of the time, which is right only 58\% of the time (right 70\% of the time on blue cards and 30\% on red, so .7 × .7 + .3 × .3); paid a nickel per correct guess over a thousand trials, they predicted blue 76\% of the time. \nb{The arithmetic is right. The 76\% could not be checked here.}
- After: I quote Robyn Dawes on an old kind of experiment. Seventy per cent of the cards are blue, in random order. Always predicting blue is right 70\% of the time. Subjects instead predict blue about 70\% of the time, which is right only 58\% of the time (right 70\% of the time on blue cards and 30\% on red, so .7 × .7 + .3 × .3). Paid a nickel per correct guess over a thousand trials, they predicted blue 76\% of the time. \nb{The arithmetic is right. The 76\% could not be checked here.}

- Before: Subjects ``just keep guessing red.'' Dawes says that despite a thousand trials of feedback they cannot believe they cannot predict. I add that they could have bet blue every time and tested their hunches in private, switching only if a hunch came true. I would not fault them for inventing hypotheses, but I would for betting on them after hundreds had failed.

I would suspect that the all-blue strategy ``just didn't occur'' to them: seeing mostly blue cards with some red, people assume the best bets must be mostly blue with some red. \nb{Experiments by Koehler and James, published in 2010, support this suspicion: most people prefer always betting on the likelier option once both strategies are described to them.}

Three times I call something ``a counterintuitive idea'': that the best strategy does not look like the cards, that one should behave lawfully amid randomness, and that one should think lawfully under uncertainty. It seems your behaviour should be as unpredictable as the world, but ``A random key does not open a random lock just because they are `both random.'\,'' You fight fire with water, not fire, and that takes an extra step of thought. On every round your partial knowledge says blue is the better bet; bet red every round and you are 100\% stupid, bet red 30\% of the time and you are 30\% stupid. ``Randomizing your actions takes you further from the target, not closer.'' \nb{Two days later, in ``The Weighted Majority Algorithm,'' Yudkowsky gave the exception: against an opponent who can predict your method, ``it is wise to choose randomly.'' That post is not in this book.}
- After: Subjects ``just keep guessing red.'' Dawes says that despite a thousand trials of feedback they cannot believe they cannot predict. They could have bet blue every time and tested their hunches in private, switching only if a hunch came true. I would not fault them for inventing hypotheses, but I would for betting on them after hundreds had failed.

I suspect that the all-blue strategy ``just didn't occur'' to them. Seeing mostly blue cards with some red, people assume the best bets must be mostly blue with some red. \nb{Experiments by Koehler and James, published in 2010, support this suspicion: most people prefer always betting on the likelier option once both strategies are described to them.}

Three times I call something ``a counterintuitive idea'': that the best strategy does not look like the cards, that one should behave lawfully amid randomness, and that one should think lawfully under uncertainty. It seems your behaviour should be as unpredictable as the world, but ``A random key does not open a random lock just because they are `both random.'\,'' You fight fire with water, not fire, and that takes an extra step of thought. On every round, your partial knowledge says blue is the better bet. Bet red every round and you are 100\% stupid. Bet red 30\% of the time and you are 30\% stupid. ``Randomizing your actions takes you further from the target, not closer.'' \nb{Two days later, in ``The Weighted Majority Algorithm,'' Yudkowsky gave the exception: against an opponent who can predict your method, ``it is wise to choose randomly.'' That post is not in this book.}

- Before: Some say, ``Rationality works fine for dealing with rational people, but the world isn't rational.'' But against an irrational opponent, throwing away your own reason does not help; decision theory still finds the best response. Every bet on red is an expected loss, ``and so too with every departure from the Way in your own thinking.'' Last, I ask how many \textsc{Star Trek} episodes, and how many theories of AI, this refutes.

- After: Some say, ``Rationality works fine for dealing with rational people, but the world isn't rational.'' But against an irrational opponent, throwing away your own reason does not help. Decision theory still finds the best response. Every bet on red is an expected loss, ``and so too with every departure from the Way in your own thinking.'' Last, I ask how many \textsc{Star Trek} episodes, and how many theories of AI, this refutes.



## honest/sections/my-wild-and-reckless-youth.tex

Why: Prose pass: split packed sentences

- Before: I was not stupid; the same errors are ``still being made today by respected scientists in respected journals.'' I name none.
- After: I was not stupid. The same errors are ``still being made today by respected scientists in respected journals.'' I name none.

- Before: Traditional Rationality is learned as an art, from the biographies of famous physicists, but you have not lived their lives, and half of what they describe is trained instinct. \nb{The post quotes no text or teacher of it.} It would have let me spend thirty years on my idea, as long as I tested it honestly and accepted the disproof in the end: enough for science to move forward, but hard on the person who wastes thirty years. It is ``a walk, not a dance.'' Its followers may agree to disagree, for it lacks the ideal of ``only one correct probability estimate given the evidence.'' It lets you guess and then test the guess, but experience has taught me that if you do not know, and you guess, you will be wrong. \nb{That ideal divides Bayesians as well. Subjective Bayesians permit any coherent starting probabilities, so two of them can disagree on the same evidence.}

## honest/sections/failing-to-learn-from-history.tex

Why: Prose pass: split packed sentences

- Before: When I gave my mysterious answer, I made many mistakes, but one was the most critical: I did not realize that ``solving a mystery should make it feel less confusing.'' I wanted to give the phenomenon a cause and fit it into my model of the world; why should that make it less mysterious, when mystery was its nature? \nb{The rule catches an answer that still feels mysterious. It does not catch one that feels like an explanation and is not, which is the error Yudkowsky had described the day before in ``Say Not Complexity.''}
- After: When I gave my mysterious answer, I made many mistakes, but one was the most critical: I did not realize that ``solving a mystery should make it feel less confusing.'' I wanted to give the phenomenon a cause and fit it into my model of the world. Why should that make it less mysterious, when mystery was its nature? \nb{The rule catches an answer that still feels mysterious. It does not catch one that feels like an explanation and is not, which is the error Yudkowsky had described the day before in ``Say Not Complexity.''}

- Before: I took the lesson of history to be that the astrologers, alchemists and vitalists had a character flaw, a taste for mystery that made them give mysterious explanations of subjects that were not mysterious. Surely a truly weird phenomenon might need a weird explanation? Only later, when I began to see the ordinary structure inside my own mystery, did I see how reasonable vitalism had seemed ``at the time,'' and how surprising the universe's answer was: life is ordinary and needs no weird explanation. \nb{The history is not told in this post; the vitalist Lord Kelvin is quoted in ``Mysterious Answers to Mysterious Questions,'' five days earlier.}
- After: I took the lesson of history to be that the astrologers, alchemists and vitalists had a character flaw, a taste for mystery that made them give mysterious explanations of subjects that were not mysterious. Surely a truly weird phenomenon might need a weird explanation? Only later, when I began to see the ordinary structure inside my own mystery, did I see how reasonable vitalism had seemed ``at the time.'' And I saw how surprising the universe's answer was: life is ordinary and needs no weird explanation. \nb{The history is not told in this post; the vitalist Lord Kelvin is quoted in ``Mysterious Answers to Mysterious Questions,'' five days earlier.}

## honest/sections/say-not-complexity.tex

Why: Prose pass: split packed sentences; removed a 'not X but Y' set-up

- Before: This is a story from when I first met Marcello, a young competitor in mathematics olympiads whom I was considering as an apprentice. I asked him how an AI might work out for itself how to solve a Rubik's Cube: not by preprogramming, but by discovering the cube's laws and inventing the idea of an ``operator,'' a reusable sequence of moves, which is the key to solving it. He said, ``Well, I think the AI needs complexity to do X, and complexity to do Y---''
- After: This is a story from when I first met Marcello, a young competitor in mathematics olympiads whom I was considering as an apprentice. I asked him how an AI might work out for itself how to solve a Rubik's Cube. The AI would not be preprogrammed. It would discover the cube's laws and invent the idea of an ``operator,'' a reusable sequence of moves, which is the key to solving it. He said, ``Well, I think the AI needs complexity to do X, and complexity to do Y---''

- Before: He answered, ``But there's got to be some amount of complexity that does it.'' Saying ``complexity'' simply felt to me like the wrong move, and it took a moment to find words for why, since we cannot deliberate in words over every step of our thinking. I asked whether he had read my essay on technical explanation. He had. I told him: ``Saying `complexity' doesn't concentrate your probability mass.'' He saw it at once: ``like `emergence.''' Now he had to think about how X might actually happen. ``Maybe this one is teachable,'' I thought.
- After: He answered, ``But there's got to be some amount of complexity that does it.'' To me, saying ``complexity'' felt like the wrong move. It took a moment to find words for why, since we cannot deliberate in words over every step of our thinking. I asked whether he had read my essay on technical explanation. He had. I told him: ``Saying `complexity' doesn't concentrate your probability mass.'' He saw it at once: ``like `emergence.''' Now he had to think about how X might actually happen. ``Maybe this one is teachable,'' I thought.

- Before: Complexity is a useful idea in its place, I concede, with proper mathematical definitions, and even informally you must judge whether a hypothesis is too complicated for its evidence, or how to simplify a design. The mistake is using the word to skip over something you do not understand. This mistake is ``an extremely common misstep, at least in my field,'' made in discussions of AI ``left and right, over and over again.'' I name no one making it, and say it is how human beings think by default.

It happens ``below the level of words,'' in ``a microthought.'' The cure: ``You have to feel which parts of your map are still blank, and more importantly, pay attention to that feeling.''
- After: Complexity is a useful idea in its place, I concede, with proper mathematical definitions. Even informally, you must judge whether a hypothesis is too complicated for its evidence, or how to simplify a design. The mistake is using the word to skip over something you do not understand. This mistake is ``an extremely common misstep, at least in my field,'' made in discussions of AI ``left and right, over and over again.'' I name no one making it, and say it is how human beings think by default.

It happens ``below the level of words,'' in ``a microthought.'' The cure is to notice the gaps: ``You have to feel which parts of your map are still blank, and more importantly, pay attention to that feeling.''

- Before: The pressure to hide gaps, I suspect, is huge in academia, where a seemingly complete model with some ``emergent phenomena'' earns more credit than a map labelled ``then a miracle occurs,'' which a journal may not even accept. It is ``even huger'' in AI startups, where admitting that you do not know how to build AI would make your life plans collapse.

If you want examples of the skip-over, watch people discuss religion, philosophy or any science they were not trained in. Marcello and I agreed to say ``magic'' for whatever we did not understand, as a reminder that the problem was still unsolved. ``Complexity'' and ``emergence'' create an illusion of understanding; ``magic'' leaves an honest placeholder.

- After: The pressure to hide gaps, I suspect, is huge in academia. There a seemingly complete model with some ``emergent phenomena'' earns more credit than a map labelled ``then a miracle occurs,'' which a journal may not even accept. It is ``even huger'' in AI startups, where admitting that you do not know how to build AI would make your life plans collapse.

But ``magic'' leaves an honest placeholder.

## honest/sections/science-as-curiosity-stopper.tex

Why: Prose pass: split packed sentences; removed a 'not X but Y' set-up

- Before: Suppose I conjured a brilliant light out of empty space on live television. Most people, I think, would be ``fairly curious.'' Now suppose I want to use this power in public without anyone becoming curious. I have a spell for that: whenever someone asks how I did it, I say ``Science!''
- After: Suppose I conjured a brilliant light out of empty space on live television. Most people, I think, would be ``fairly curious.'' Now suppose I want to use this power in public without anyone becoming curious. I have a spell for that. Whenever someone asks how I did it, I say ``Science!''

- Before: Someone will say that an unexplained bulb could bring fame and fortune. I am not talking about greed but about the feeling of curiosity. Why should yours shrink because someone else knows? ``Is this not spite?'' For other goods knowledge serves, such as useful technology, it does matter whether someone already knows; for curiosity, why should it? \nb{The post gives no evidence of spite, and does not consider the reason in the previous note.}

If ``someone else knows'' could stop curiosity, then a voice saying ``Somebody already knows why this elephant is here'' would let you walk past a hovering green elephant. \nb{The elephant would still grip you because it fits nothing you know. That is the reason given two notes earlier.} I do not know the final laws of physics, and I do not know where my kidneys are; why should the first intrigue me more than the second? I bet you do not know how your cerebellum, ``among other brain areas,'' coordinates a wave of your hand, so why should a light spell intrigue you more than raising your hand? I call this a ``double standard.'' Look in the mirror: do you know what looks out from behind your eyes, how your knees work, how your shoes were made, why water is wet? Some of this science knows and some it does not, but why should that matter to your curiosity if you do not know?
- After: Someone will say that an unexplained bulb could bring fame and fortune. But my subject is the feeling of curiosity. Why should yours shrink because someone else knows? ``Is this not spite?'' For other goods knowledge serves, such as useful technology, it does matter whether someone already knows. For curiosity, why should it? \nb{The post gives no evidence of spite, and does not consider the reason in the previous note.}

If ``someone else knows'' could stop curiosity, then a voice saying ``Somebody already knows why this elephant is here'' would let you walk past a hovering green elephant. \nb{The elephant would still grip you because it fits nothing you know. I bet you do not know how your cerebellum, ``among other brain areas,'' coordinates a wave of your hand, so why should a light spell intrigue you more than raising your hand? I call this a ``double standard.'' Look in the mirror: do you know what looks out from behind your eyes, how your knees work, how your shoes were made, why water is wet? Some of this science knows and some it does not, but why should that matter to your curiosity if you do not know?


## honest/sections/truly-part-of-you.tex

Why: Prose pass: split packed sentences

- Before: A person who believes ``light is waves'' because a physicist said so has the same kind of node in their head. As McDermott says, ``The whole problem is getting the hearer to notice what it has been told.'' \nb{In McDermott's paper that sentence is about computer programs passing data to each other, a case the paper contrasts with human speech. The post applies it to a person.} If the physicist had said ``Light is made of little curvy things,'' would you notice any difference in what you expect? My test: ``How would I regenerate this knowledge if it were deleted from my mind?'' A machine that stores ``Plus-Of(Seven, Six) = Thirteen'' cannot get it back once deleted, until a human types it in again; if you forgot that light is waves, you could only ask the physicist again, not work it out as physicists did.

Experience connects a belief to other knowledge, to perception and to action. Someone who has only been told facts about ``beavers'' ``may not be able to recognize a beaver'' when they see one. I cite Davidson: if you believe beavers live in deserts, are pure white and weigh 300 pounds, you have no beliefs about beavers at all. \nb{That example concerns beliefs that are mostly false, not true beliefs taken on trust. The article the post cites, by Richard Rorty, explains it by the view that words get their meaning from shared use among speakers, ``not by being paired off with particular experiences or objects.''} This is the danger of telling an AI facts it could not learn for itself, and of telling people physics they cannot check: what physicists mean by ``wave'' is a mathematical concept, not a squiggly thing. Then Wittgenstein: ``A wheel that can be turned though nothing else moves with it, is not part of the mechanism.'' \nb{In Wittgenstein's passage the idle wheel is a private meaning, in a man who ``uses it as we all do.''}

I have asked myself this question since I first read about AI. Could I re-prove the Pythagorean theorem if I forgot the proofs? ``I think so.'' Would I notice the theorem if I forgot it entirely? That is ``harder to boast,'' but if you handed me a 3-4 right triangle and told me the hypotenuse could be calculated, ``I think I would be able to calculate it.'' \nb{In that version someone else notices the problem for the author, which was the part to be tested. The post describes no case in which the test was run and found a belief wanting.} Could I reinvent the very idea of a mathematical proof, which someone once had to invent? The test is not only for weeding out loose beliefs; it is a way to take in ``a fountain of knowledge, not just one fact.''
- After: A person who believes ``light is waves'' because a physicist said so has the same kind of node in their head. As McDermott says, ``The whole problem is getting the hearer to notice what it has been told.'' \nb{In McDermott's paper that sentence is about computer programs passing data to each other, a case the paper contrasts with human speech. The post applies it to a person.} If the physicist had said ``Light is made of little curvy things,'' would you notice any difference in what you expect? My test: ``How would I regenerate this knowledge if it were deleted from my mind?'' A machine that stores ``Plus-Of(Seven, Six) = Thirteen'' cannot get it back once deleted, until a human types it in again. If you forgot that light is waves, you could only ask the physicist again. You could not work it out as physicists did.
